It just sort of... slips away.\n\nDoctor: When your wife is talking, do you ever have trouble hearing the words clearly?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's not my hearing at all. Actually, when this first started becoming a real point of contention between us, I thought, well, maybe I'm just getting older and my ears are going. My dad had terrible hearing by the time he was my age—he worked around heavy machinery, you know, the old metal stamping presses back in the day, and he never wore ear protection because they just didn't really push safety standards back then like they do now. \n\nSo, just to be safe, I actually went down to the clinic and had my hearing checked a little while back. I sat in that tiny soundproof booth with the heavy headphones on, pressing the little button every time I heard a beep. And the audiologist came out and told me my hearing was practically perfect for a man my age. Honestly, I can hear a pin drop. I can hear the refrigerator humming all the way from the living room, which actually drives me a bit crazy sometimes when I'm just trying to sit quietly and read the morning paper. \n\nSo, no, when my wife speaks, the volume is fine, the clarity of the sound hitting my eardrum is perfectly fine. I hear the syllables, the tone of her voice, the pitch, everything. It's just... it's like the bridge between my ears and my brain is temporarily out of service. The words go in, but they don't get unpacked, if that makes sense? I'm not deaf to the sound of her voice at all, I'm just... absent for the meaning of it.\n\nDoctor: If you read the same information on paper instead of hearing it, does it usually stick better?\n\nPatient: Well, you'd think so, wouldn't you? That's what I thought, too. I figured, if I can just look at it, if it's written down right there in black and white, it'll stay put. But honestly, it's not much better. \n\nLike, for example, I help out with the parish council down at St. Jude's. Have for years. And occasionally I'll help coordinate the catering for some of the bigger events, like the spring fish fry or the fall festival. So, I'll sit down at the kitchen table with the catering menus and the order sheets, and I'll have my reading glasses on, a fresh cup of coffee, completely ready to tackle it. I'll start reading through the options—how many pounds of coleslaw we need, the pricing for the tartar sauce packets, all that. And I start off fine. I'm reading the words, I'm understanding the prices. \n\nBut then, I don't know, maybe five minutes in, my eyes are still moving across the page, line by line, but my mind is... it's just gone. I'll be thinking about the gutters that need cleaning, or wondering if I remembered to put the recycling bin out, or just thinking about absolutely nothing at all. And then I'll look down and realize I've \"read\" three whole paragraphs about potato salad, but I have no earthly idea what any of it said. I have to go back to the top of the page and start all over again, reading the exact same notes like I never even looked at them the first time. \n\nIt happens on the planning calls, too, when we do those conference calls. I'll be taking notes, trying to write things down so they stick, but my mind just drifts off right in the middle of someone explaining the volunteer schedule. So, whether it's spoken or written down, if I have to focus on it for more than a few minutes, it just slips right through my fingers.\n\nDoctor: Looking back to when you were a child in school, did you often have the same kind of trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it’s funny you ask about that, because I actually got to thinking about this very thing the other day. I was digging through some old boxes in the attic—my wife has been on me to clear out some of the clutter up there, and I found a box of my old report cards and keepsakes my mother had saved from when I was a boy. And looking at those report cards from, gosh, third grade, fourth grade, right up through sixth grade, so well before I was twelve... it was like reading a script of exactly what I just described to you. \n\nMy teachers—Mrs. Gable in fourth grade, I remember her specifically, she was a wonderful woman, very patient, but she used to write these long notes to my parents. She’d say, you know, \"He’s a very bright boy, he has so much potential,\" but then there would always be the \"but.\" \"But he spends half the day staring out the window daydreaming.\" Or, \"He forgot his spelling homework in his desk again for the third time this week.\" \n\nAnd it wasn't just the homework. Even when I was sitting right there in the classroom, with the assignment right in front of me, Mrs. Gable or Mr. Henderson in fifth grade, they were constantly having to tap my desk or call my name to remind me to actually finish the classwork. I’d start it, do the first two math problems, and then my mind would just wander off to what I was going to do at recess, or watching a bird outside, or just getting lost in my own thoughts. So, yes, looking back on it, that same sort of drift, that inability to just stay locked onto what I was supposed to be doing... it’s been there pretty much my whole life, even way back then. I guess I just always thought of it as a personality quirk, you know? Just being a bit of a space cadet. But those old report cards, they really laid it out in black and white.\n\nDoctor: Before you retired, did this same trouble staying focused create problems for you at work?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's interesting you ask about work, because honestly, I retired a few years ago, mostly because I just felt like I was slowing down, but looking back, I think a lot of it was this... this exact same thing. But where it's really showing up now, where it's actually becoming a real problem, isn't so much a traditional job, but the responsibilities I have now. \n\nYou see, my cousin, Arthur, he's been having some health issues lately, quite severe actually, and my wife and I have been helping out with his care. We take turns going over there, making sure he's taken his medications, getting his meals sorted, that sort of thing. And it has started causing some real tension at home between me and my wife, because she feels like she just can't count on me to handle it. Like, last Tuesday, she asked me to make sure Arthur took his afternoon pills and to write down his blood pressure reading. And I went over there, we had a nice chat, I made him a sandwich, and I came home. And my wife asked, \"What was his blood pressure?\" And I just drew a complete blank. I hadn't even thought about the pills, either. I was there, I was doing things, but the actual important tasks just completely evaporated from my mind. She was so frustrated with me, and I don't blame her, I really don't, because it's a serious matter, Arthur's health. \n\nAnd it's not just with Arthur, either. Like I mentioned earlier with the parish council and the catering—I've had to double-check or completely redo so many of those tasks lately. I'll be in charge of ordering the napkins and the plastic cutlery for the fall festival, a tiny, simple job, right? But I'll forget if I placed the order, or I'll order the wrong amount because I wasn't paying attention when I read the inventory sheet, and then I have to spend an hour on the phone with the supplier trying to fix it so these small jobs don't get completely mixed up and ruin the event. \n\nSo, I guess to answer your question, it's not a nine-to-five job anymore, but the \"work\" I am doing now, the things people are relying on me for... it's definitely creating problems. My wife is just at her wit's end with it, feeling like she has to manage me on top of managing Arthur's care and the household, and I hate making her feel that way, I really do. It's just so frustrating to know you're capable of doing a task, but your brain just won't stay on the tracks long enough to get to the station.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say so. You know, it's funny you ask that, because if anything, I might be the exact opposite. My wife, she’s always telling me I’m like a bump on a log once I sit down. I have this old leather recliner in the den—had it for, oh, must be fifteen years now, comfortable as anything, perfectly broken in right at the lower back—and once I settle into that chair after lunch to watch a bit of the news or maybe one of those historical documentaries, I am perfectly content to just sit there for hours without moving a muscle. \n\nI really don't get that urge to get up and pace around, and I'm certainly not bouncing my leg up and down or tapping my fingers on the armrest. I have a nephew, Jimmy, my sister's boy, and he does that—he just cannot sit still for two minutes without jiggling his knee or drumming his fingers on the table. It drives his mother absolutely up the wall, especially at Thanksgiving dinner when we're all trying to sit and have a nice, quiet meal. But no, that's never been my problem. \n\nMy body stays put just fine. I can sit perfectly still at the kitchen table when I'm trying to look at those catering menus, or in the pew at church, or in my chair at home. It's just my mind that's wandering off to Timbuktu. Physically, though? No, I wouldn't say I'm restless or fidgety at all. I'm actually quite relaxed, physically speaking. Maybe almost too relaxed sometimes, if you ask my wife when she’s trying to get me out the door to go run errands!\n\nDoctor: Have these attention problems gotten noticeably worse in the past few years compared with how you were earlier in adulthood?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s interesting you phrase it that way, comparing it to earlier in adulthood. Because, like I said, I’ve always been a bit of a space cadet, always had that tendency to drift off. Throughout my thirties and forties, you know, I managed it. I had my routines, my wife kept the house organized, and I got by just fine. But has it gotten worse? Yes. Absolutely. \n\nBut it wasn't just some slow, creeping thing over the last few years. It really spiked quite recently. I'd say... well, it's been noticeably harder for about eleven months now. Exactly eleven months, actually, because we just passed the anniversary of the whole ordeal. You see, that cousin I mentioned earlier, Arthur? Well, about eleven months ago, he had to have a pretty major surgery. It was supposed to be a standard procedure, but there were complications, and the recovery was just grueling for him. He was living in this older split-level house over in the Heights, stairs everywhere, just completely impractical for someone trying to get around on a walker after an operation like that. \n\nSo, my wife and I, we sat down and talked it over, and we decided the only sensible thing to do was to have him move in with us while he recovered. We have a nice guest room on the first floor, right next to a full bath, so it made perfect sense on paper. But let me tell you, ever since he moved in with us after that surgery, my ability to keep my thoughts straight has just gone completely out the window. \n\nIt's like the extra layer of things to remember—the physical therapists coming and going on Tuesdays and Thursdays, the new dietary restrictions my wife has us all on for Arthur's sake, all the little disruptions to the quiet routine we used to have—it just completely overloaded my circuits. Before he moved in, if my mind wandered while I was reading the paper or fixing a hinge on a cabinet, it wasn't the end of the world. I'd just circle back to it eventually. But these past eleven months, with him living right there in the house with us and needing so much coordination, it’s been so much more difficult to just stay focused. The stakes are higher, and my brain just feels like it's scattering in a dozen different directions every time I try to sit down and focus on a task for him. So, yes, it's definitely gotten worse, but it really traces right back to when he moved in with us eleven months ago.\n\nDoctor: Over the past eleven months, have you been feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Depressed? No, no, I really wouldn’t use that word. I mean, don't get me wrong, it’s been stressful, like I was just saying, with Arthur being here and all the extra commotion in the house. And it is incredibly frustrating when I forget things, and I do feel terrible when my wife gets upset with me because I’ve dropped the ball on something important. It weighs on you, sure, feeling like you're letting people down. \n\nBut actually feeling depressed? Down in the dumps, sad, hopeless? No, not at all. If anything, my mood is generally pretty good. I still really enjoy my days when I'm not tripping over my own forgetfulness. Just the other afternoon, for example, I was out in the garage—I like to tinker a bit, mostly just light woodworking, birdhouses, fixing up old chairs, that sort of thing—and I was perfectly happy out there. The weather was beautiful, we finally got a break from that humidity we were having all last week, I had the radio tuned to the classic rock station, and I felt just fine. I still have a good appetite, I still look forward to my morning coffee out on the back porch with the newspaper, even if I can't always get through a full article without my mind wandering off to think about the gutters or the lawn. \n\nSo, no, it's definitely not a mood thing. I'm not sitting around crying or feeling like life is bleak. It's really just the pure frustration of my brain not cooperating with the tasks I need to do. My wife actually asked me that exact same thing a few months ago. She said, \"Are you depressed?\" because I was sighing so much around the house, but I told her, \"I'm not sad, honey, I'm just annoyed that I forgot why I walked into the kitchen for the third time today.\" So, no, definitely not depressed. Just... scattered. Very, very scattered.\n\nDoctor: Over the past eleven months, have you been feeling more anxious or worried than usual?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Worried? Well... hmm. I suppose it depends on how you define it. Am I sitting around wringing my hands, terrified that the sky is falling or that something terrible is going to happen to my family? No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not a nervous wreck in that sense. I don't get panic attacks or feel my heart racing out of my chest for no reason. \n\nBut... am I worried? Yes, I suppose I am, but it's a very specific kind of worry. It's not a general anxiety about the world, it's a constant, nagging worry about *myself*. About my own brain. Ever since Arthur moved in and things got so chaotic, I just have this underlying hum of anxiety about what I'm going to forget next. It's like I'm always waiting for the other shoe to drop. I'll be sitting there, watching a baseball game—say, the Astros are playing, and it's the bottom of the seventh, a really tense moment—and instead of just enjoying the game, this thought will just pop into my head: \"Did I remember to call the pharmacy to refill Arthur's blood pressure medication?\" And then my heart will sink a little bit, and I'll have to get up and check, and usually, I haven't forgotten, but sometimes I have. \n\nAnd it's that *sometimes* that really gets to me. It makes you second-guess yourself constantly. I used to trust my own mind, you know? If I said I was going to do something, I did it. But now, I'm just so worried that I'm going to let my wife down, or worse, that I'm going to mess up something important for Arthur's recovery. The other day, my wife asked me to pick up some specific low-sodium chicken broth from the grocery store—the one in the blue carton, not the yellow one—and I was so anxious about forgetting it that I repeated \"blue carton, blue carton\" the entire drive there. And you know what? I still ended up standing in the soup aisle for ten minutes staring at the shelves because I started second-guessing whether she said blue carton or blue label. \n\nSo, yes, I am worried, but it's a worry born out of frustration. It's the anxiety of not being able to rely on your own memory to just get through a normal day without causing a problem for everyone else. It's exhausting, honestly. Just constantly being on guard against my own forgetfulness. But it's not like a clinical anxiety disorder, I don't think. It's just the natural reaction to feeling like your brain is a sieve.\n\nDoctor: Outside of losing focus, do you find yourself forgetting recent conversations or events even when you were paying attention at the time?\n\nPatient: Well, now, that’s a very interesting distinction to make, and I’m glad you asked it exactly that way. Because my wife, bless her heart, she sometimes gets it into her head that my memory is just completely failing me, like I'm getting senile early or something. But I always have to stop her and say, \"Hold on, it’s not that the file got deleted from the hard drive, it’s that it was never saved in the first place!\" \n\nIf I am actually dialed in—if I'm looking right at you, and I’m actively engaged in what we’re doing—my memory is honestly like a steel trap. I don't forget things that I've actually absorbed. Just this past Tuesday, for instance. Or wait, was it Wednesday? No, it was Tuesday, because that’s one of the days Arthur’s physical therapist comes to the house. This young guy, Mark, very polite kid, went to Texas A&M. Anyway, Mark was explaining this entirely new stretching routine for Arthur's knee. And because I knew it was absolutely critical that we get it right so we don't set back his recovery, I made a very conscious effort. I put down my coffee mug on the side table, I turned my chair to face Mark directly, and I just locked in. \n\nAnd you know what? I can tell you right now, days later, exactly what he said word for word. He said we need to elevate Arthur's leg to exactly a forty-five-degree angle, hold it for fifteen seconds—not twenty, fifteen—and make absolutely sure his heel is flexed toward the ceiling the entire time. I remembered every single detail of that conversation perfectly, without writing it down, because I was truly paying attention. \n\nThe trouble only rears its head when my mind starts drifting. If my wife is telling me about her sister's new patio furniture while I’m standing there trying to calculate if it’s been three months since I last changed the air filter in the HVAC system... well, then, sure, ten minutes later I won't have a clue what color cushions her sister bought. But that’s simply because I wasn't really listening to her in the first place, you see? I was miles away, thinking about fiberglass filters and dust buildup. So, no, to answer your question, if I am truly paying attention and focused on the moment, I don't forget conversations or events at all. The machinery up there still works fine, it’s just... it's just getting my brain to stay tuned to the right channel long enough to record the program, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: During the past eleven months, have you been worrying excessively about many different things, not just your forgetfulness and Arthur’s care?\n\nPatient: No, no, not at all. Like I was trying to explain just a minute ago, it’s really just that one specific thing. I’m honestly not a worrier by nature. Never have been, really. I mean, my wife, she’s the one who can fret about the electric bill going up a few dollars in the summer, or whether the neighbor's big oak tree is leaning a little too close to our property line after a bad thunderstorm. But me? I’ve always been the type to just take things as they come. Especially since I stopped working, I really don't have those big, looming, everyday stresses hanging over my head. \n\nJust last month, for example, the check engine light came on in my truck. Now, a lot of guys would lose sleep over that, right? They'd be lying awake thinking about the transmission failing, or how much the dealership is going to charge for parts, because you know you can't just get under the hood with a basic wrench anymore. Everything is all computerized and sealed up with plastic covers, so they charge you an arm and a leg just to plug it into their machine. But I just looked at the dashboard and thought, well, I'll take it down to the shop on Tuesday and whatever it is, it is. I didn't lose a single wink of sleep over it. I just don't have that kind of... generalized panic about life, you know? \n\nSo, no, my worries are very, very localized. If Arthur wasn't staying with us right now, and if I wasn't constantly dropping the ball on my daily tasks because my focus keeps drifting off into the clouds, I'd probably be the most relaxed guy on the block. It's really just the frustration of my own brain letting me down that gets my heart rate up. The rest of the world? The economy, the house maintenance, the news? Nah. I just let all that stuff roll right off my back."
  }

Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. Actually, when this first started becoming a real point of contention between us, I thought, well, maybe I'm just getting older and my ears are going. My dad had terrible hearing by the time he was my age—he worked around heavy machinery, you know, the old metal stamping presses back in the day, and he never wore ear protection because they just didn't really push safety standards back then like they do now. \n\nSo, just to be safe, I actually went down to the clinic and had my hearing checked a little while back. I sat in that tiny soundproof booth with the heavy headphones on, pressing the little button every time I heard a beep. And the audiologist came out and told me my hearing was practically perfect for a man my age. Honestly, I can hear a pin drop. I can hear the refrigerator humming all the way from the living room, which actually drives me a bit crazy sometimes when I'm just trying to sit quietly and read the morning paper. \n\nSo, no, when my wife speaks, the volume is fine, the clarity of the sound hitting my eardrum is perfectly fine. I hear the syllables, the tone of her voice, the pitch, everything. It's just... it's like the bridge between my ears and my brain is temporarily out of service. The words go in, but they don't get unpacked, if that makes sense? I'm not deaf to the sound of her voice at all, I'm just... absent for the meaning of it.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

  "confirmed": [
    "S003",
    "S008"
  ],
  "denied": [],

Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, you'd think so, wouldn't you? That's what I thought, too. I figured, if I can just look at it, if it's written down right there in black and white, it'll stay put. But honestly, it's not much better. \n\nLike, for example, I help out with the parish council down at St. Jude's. Have for years. And occasionally I'll help coordinate the catering for some of the bigger events, like the spring fish fry or the fall festival. So, I'll sit down at the kitchen table with the catering menus and the order sheets, and I'll have my reading glasses on, a fresh cup of coffee, completely ready to tackle it. I'll start reading through the options—how many pounds of coleslaw we need, the pricing for the tartar sauce packets, all that. And I start off fine. I'm reading the words, I'm understanding the prices. \n\nBut then, I don't know, maybe five minutes in, my eyes are still moving across the page, line by line, but my mind is... it's just gone. I'll be thinking about the gutters that need cleaning, or wondering if I remembered to put the recycling bin out, or just thinking about absolutely nothing at all. And then I'll look down and realize I've \"read\" three whole paragraphs about potato salad, but I have no earthly idea what any of it said. I have to go back to the top of the page and start all over again, reading the exact same notes like I never even looked at them the first time. \n\nIt happens on the planning calls, too, when we do those conference calls. I'll be taking notes, trying to write things down so they stick, but my mind just drifts off right in the middle of someone explaining the volunteer schedule. So, whether it's spoken or written down, if I have to focus on it for more than a few minutes, it just slips right through my fingers.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }

Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep). Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry. Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, you know, it's interesting you ask about work, because honestly, I retired a few years ago, mostly because I just felt like I was slowing down, but looking back, I think a lot of it was this... this exact same thing. But where it's really showing up now, where it's actually becoming a real problem, isn't so much a traditional job, but the responsibilities I have now. \n\nYou see, my cousin, Arthur, he's been having some health issues lately, quite severe actually, and my wife and I have been helping out with his care. We take turns going over there, making sure he's taken his medications, getting his meals sorted, that sort of thing. And it has started causing some real tension at home between me and my wife, because she feels like she just can't count on me to handle it. Like, last Tuesday, she asked me to make sure Arthur took his afternoon pills and to write down his blood pressure reading. And I went over there, we had a nice chat, I made him a sandwich, and I came home. And my wife asked, \"What was his blood pressure?\" And I just drew a complete blank. I hadn't even thought about the pills, either. I was there, I was doing things, but the actual important tasks just completely evaporated from my mind. She was so frustrated with me, and I don't blame her, I really don't, because it's a serious matter, Arthur's health. \n\nAnd it's not just with Arthur, either. Like I mentioned earlier with the parish council and the catering—I've had to double-check or completely redo so many of those tasks lately. I'll be in charge of ordering the napkins and the plastic cutlery for the fall festival, a tiny, simple job, right? But I'll forget if I placed the order, or I'll order the wrong amount because I wasn't paying attention when I read the inventory sheet, and then I have to spend an hour on the phone with the supplier trying to fix it so these small jobs don't get completely mixed up and ruin the event. \n\nSo, I guess to answer your question, it's not a nine-to-five job anymore, but the \"work\" I am doing now, the things people are relying on me for... it's definitely creating problems. My wife is just at her wit's end with it, feeling like she has to manage me on top of managing Arthur's care and the household, and I hate making her feel that way, I really do. It's just so frustrating to know you're capable of doing a task, but your brain just won't stay on the tracks long enough to get to the station.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

    "S008": "The patient describes a lifelong 'tendency to drift off' and mentions his 'mind wandered' while doing activities like reading or fixing things.",

    "S081": "The patient clearly links the recent severe worsening of his cognitive and focus symptoms to the identifiable stressor of his cousin moving in after surgery eleven months ago."
  }

Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. She said, \"Are you depressed?\" because I was sighing so much around the house, but I told her, \"I'm not sad, honey, I'm just annoyed that I forgot why I walked into the kitchen for the third time today.\" So, no, definitely not depressed. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nAnxious? Worried? Well... hmm. I suppose it depends on how you define it. Am I sitting around wringing my hands, terrified that the sky is falling or that something terrible is going to happen to my family? No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not a nervous wreck in that sense. I don't get panic attacks or feel my heart racing out of my chest for no reason. \n\nBut... am I worried? Yes, I suppose I am, but it's a very specific kind of worry. It's not a general anxiety about the world, it's a constant, nagging worry about *myself*. About my own brain. Ever since Arthur moved in and things got so chaotic, I just have this underlying hum of anxiety about what I'm going to forget next. It's like I'm always waiting for the other shoe to drop. I'll be sitting there, watching a baseball game—say, the Astros are playing, and it's the bottom of the seventh, a really tense moment—and instead of just enjoying the game, this thought will just pop into my head: \"Did I remember to call the pharmacy to refill Arthur's blood pressure medication?\" And then my heart will sink a little bit, and I'll have to get up and check, and usually, I haven't forgotten, but sometimes I have. \n\nAnd it's that *sometimes* that really gets to me. It makes you second-guess yourself constantly. I used to trust my own mind, you know? If I said I was going to do something, I did it. But now, I'm just so worried that I'm going to let my wife down, or worse, that I'm going to mess up something important for Arthur's recovery. The other day, my wife asked me to pick up some specific low-sodium chicken broth from the grocery store—the one in the blue carton, not the yellow one—and I was so anxious about forgetting it that I repeated \"blue carton, blue carton\" the entire drive there. And you know what? I still ended up standing in the soup aisle for ten minutes staring at the shelves because I started second-guessing whether she said blue carton or blue label. \n\nSo, yes, I am worried, but it's a worry born out of frustration. It's the anxiety of not being able to rely on your own memory to just get through a normal day without causing a problem for everyone else. It's exhausting, honestly. Just constantly being on guard against my own forgetfulness. But it's not like a clinical anxiety disorder, I don't think. It's just the natural reaction to feeling like your brain is a sieve.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, now, that’s a very interesting distinction to make, and I’m glad you asked it exactly that way. Because my wife, bless her heart, she sometimes gets it into her head that my memory is just completely failing me, like I'm getting senile early or something. But I always have to stop her and say, \"Hold on, it’s not that the file got deleted from the hard drive, it’s that it was never saved in the first place!\" \n\nIf I am actually dialed in—if I'm looking right at you, and I’m actively engaged in what we’re doing—my memory is honestly like a steel trap. I don't forget things that I've actually absorbed. Just this past Tuesday, for instance. Or wait, was it Wednesday? No, it was Tuesday, because that’s one of the days Arthur’s physical therapist comes to the house. This young guy, Mark, very polite kid, went to Texas A&M. Anyway, Mark was explaining this entirely new stretching routine for Arthur's knee. And because I knew it was absolutely critical that we get it right so we don't set back his recovery, I made a very conscious effort. I put down my coffee mug on the side table, I turned my chair to face Mark directly, and I just locked in. \n\nAnd you know what? I can tell you right now, days later, exactly what he said word for word. He said we need to elevate Arthur's leg to exactly a forty-five-degree angle, hold it for fifteen seconds—not twenty, fifteen—and make absolutely sure his heel is flexed toward the ceiling the entire time. I remembered every single detail of that conversation perfectly, without writing it down, because I was truly paying attention. \n\nThe trouble only rears its head when my mind starts drifting. If my wife is telling me about her sister's new patio furniture while I’m standing there trying to calculate if it’s been three months since I last changed the air filter in the HVAC system... well, then, sure, ten minutes later I won't have a clue what color cushions her sister bought. But that’s simply because I wasn't really listening to her in the first place, you see? I was miles away, thinking about fiberglass filters and dust buildup. So, no, to answer your question, if I am truly paying attention and focused on the moment, I don't forget conversations or events at all. The machinery up there still works fine, it’s just... it's just getting my brain to stay tuned to the right channel long enough to record the program, if that makes any sense.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
